=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
Fennec and Snuke are playing a board game.
You are given a positive integer N and a sequence A=(A_1,A_2,\dots,A_N) of positive integers of length N. Also, there is a set S, which is initially empty.
Fennec and Snuke take turns performing the following operation in order, starting with Fennec.

- Choose an index i such that 1\leq A_i. Subtract 1 from A_i, and if i\notin S, add i to S.
- If S=\lbrace 1,2,\dots,N \rbrace, the game ends and the player who performed the last operation wins.

Note that it can be proven that until a winner is determined and the game ends, players can always make a move (there exists some i such that 1\leq A_i). 
Both Fennec and Snuke play optimally to win. Determine who will win.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
A_1 A_2 \dots A_N

Output

Print Fennec if Fennec wins, or Snuke if Snuke wins.
The judge is case-insensitive; for example, if the correct answer is Fennec, outputs such as fennec, FENNEC, or fEnNeC will also be accepted.

Constraints


- 1\leq N\leq 2\times 10^5
- 1\leq A_i\leq 10^9 (1\leq i\leq N)
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

3
1 9 2

Sample Output 1

Fennec

For example, the game may proceed as follows:

- Initially, A=(1,9,2) and S is empty.
- Fennec chooses index 2. Then, A=(1,8,2) and S=\lbrace 2 \rbrace.
- Snuke chooses index 2. Then, A=(1,7,2) and S=\lbrace 2 \rbrace.
- Fennec chooses index 1. Then, A=(0,7,2) and S=\lbrace 1,2 \rbrace.
- Snuke chooses index 2. Then, A=(0,6,2) and S=\lbrace 1,2 \rbrace.
- Fennec chooses index 3. Then, A=(0,6,1) and S=\lbrace 1,2,3 \rbrace. The game ends with Fennec declared the winner.

This sequence of moves may not be optimal; however, it can be shown that even when both players play optimally, Fennec will win.

Sample Input 2

2
25 29

Sample Output 2

Snuke

Sample Input 3

6
1 9 2 25 2 9

Sample Output 3

Snuke

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's think strategically. Since a player can always choose to visit an unvisited index; passes are optional. The game ends when last unvisited index is visited. - first visits of N-1 non-last indices: N-1 moves
- extra moves of non-last indices that are actually played: between 0 and sum_{i≠L}(A_i-1)
- first visit of last index: 1 move (the final move)

The final move is made by player 1 (Fennec) if T is odd, player 2 if T even. Players can choose X by playing extra moves or not, but both have control. The game is essentially a finite game where players can decide to take extra moves to adjust parity; each extra move available after an index is first visited. Since extra moves are just passes, the parity of T can be controlled by the player who wants to win, provided enough extra moves and turn positions.

Let's derive using only losing/winning.

Let L_k(p,e) be losing boolean for state with k unvisited, p odd weights, pass parity e. For k>=4, conjecture L_k(p,e) = [e == (p+k) mod 2]. Let's denote parity values.

For a move:
- Pass (if e>0): leads to (k,p,1-e). By conjecture, this state is losing iff (1-e) == (p+k) mod2, i.e., e != (p+k) mod2. Thus if current e == (p+k) mod2, pass leads to winning (good for losing condition). If current e != (p+k) mod2, pass leads to losing (so current would be winning if pass available). Therefore for L to hold, we need e == (p+k) mod2 and e>0? If e=0 no pass. If e != formula, pass is a winning move, so state is N. This matches conjecture: L only when e == formula. Good.

- Remove a token. Need resulting state (k-1, p', e') be winning for opponent. There are two types:
   * Remove even weight (if p < k): p' = p, e' = e (since even w doesn't change E parity). Resulting state is losing (for player to move, opponent) iff e == (p + (k-1)) mod2 = (p+k+1) mod2 (since -1 same +1). For current e == (p+k) mod2, e != (p+k+1) mod2, so resulting state is winning. Good.
   * Remove odd weight (if p > 0): p' = p-1, e' = e xor 1. Resulting losing iff (e xor 1) == ((p-1) + (k-1)) mod2 = (p+k) mod2? Compute (p-1+k-1)=p+k-2 ≡ p+k mod2. So losing iff e xor 1 == p+k mod2, i.e., e != p+k mod2. If current e == p+k mod2, resulting state is winning. Good.

Thus if current e == (p+k) mod2, all moves (pass and removals) lead to winning states, so state is losing. If current e != (p+k) mod2:
- Pass (if e>0) leads to losing, so state is winning. If e=0 (so e != formula means formula=1), no pass. Need check if there exists a removal leading to losing. Since e=0, formula =1 (p+k odd). Need find a removal such that resulting state losing. For even removal: resulting losing iff e == (p+k+1) mod2 = 0? Since p+k odd, p+k+1 even, e=0 yes. So if there is an even weight (p<k), removing it leads to losing, so state winning. If no even weight (p=k), then p+k = 2k even, but formula=1 impossible. So if e=0 and formula=1, p+k odd, so p<k (at least one even). Thus winning move exists. If e=1 and formula=0 (p+k even), pass exists (e>0) leads to losing, so winning. Thus conjecture holds.

Let's perhaps formalize the induction for our own confidence:
For k>=4, define P(k,p,e): losing iff e = p+k mod2.
Base k=4 verified by direct case analysis using k=3 table.
Inductive step:
Assume for k-1>=4. For state (k,p,e):
If e = p+k mod2:
- Pass (if e>0): new e' = 1-e. Need show new state is winning. New condition for losing at same k would require 1-e = p+k mod2, false. But wait we cannot use the condition for same k in the proof of same k? We are proving by induction on k, so for pass move, k is same, not k-1. We need know outcome of (k,p,1-e) to determine if pass leads to losing. This is not covered by induction on k. Oops! In recurrence, pass move keeps k, so to prove L condition for k, we need know outcome for same k with opposite e. This is a mutual recursion between e=0 and e=1. Our earlier SG equations solved pairs. The simple induction on k with pass move is not straightforward because pass move doesn't reduce k. We need handle the two parities simultaneously or prove by induction on E? But we observed periodicity. Need be careful: In the losing condition proof, if current e = formula, pass leads to e' != formula. To assert e' state is winning, we need know that for same k, states with e != formula are winning. That is part of what we're proving. We can prove the pair of statements simultaneously:
For all k>=4:
(A) If e = (p+k) mod2 then losing.
(B) If e != (p+k) mod2 then winning.
We can prove by induction on k, using both A and B for same k? For pass move, from A (e=formula) pass leads to e' != formula, which is B for same k. But B for same k is also being proven. This is a circularity unless we use induction on something else or solve the two equations simultaneously.

However, in the actual game, from a state with e=formula, a pass move leads to e' != formula. To show that state is winning, we need exhibit a winning move from it. That winning move might be a pass back? If e' >0 (which it is if e=1? Wait e' = 1-e. If e=0, pass not available. If e=1, pass to e=0. To show e'=0 state is winning, we can use a token removal move, not pass, because e'=0 no pass. So we can prove B for e'=0 by finding a token removal to a losing k-1 state, using induction. For e'=1, to show winning, pass to e=0 which is losing by A? But A for e=0 is part of same k. Hmm.

Let's examine. The recurrence for outcome can be solved for the two parities simultaneously because pass move just swaps e. We can define L_e and W_e. For a fixed k and p, the equations are:
L_e = (no pass or pass to W_{1-e}) AND (all removals lead to W_{k-1}).
W_e = (pass to L_{1-e} if e>0) OR (exists removal to L_{k-1}).
Actually a state is winning if there exists at least one move to a losing state. Moves include pass if e>0 and removals. So W_e = (e>0 and L_{1-e}) OR (exists removal leading to L_{k-1}).
L_e = not W_e.

Thus to prove L_e, we need:
- If e>0, L_{1-e} is false (i.e., W_{1-e} true).
- No removal to L_{k-1}.
This couples L_e and L_{1-e}. But we can solve the boolean equations for L_e and L_{1-e} using the removal options. The induction on k gives L_{k-1} for all e, so the removal options are known. Then the equations for L_e and L_{1-e} are two equations in two unknowns. We can solve them and show the unique solution is L_e = [e=formula]. This is what we did in SG computations. So the proof is valid if we solve the pair.

Let's verify the pair solution for k>=4 using induction:
Assume for k-1, L_{k-1}(p',e') = [e' = (p'+k-1) mod2].
For fixed k,p, let f = (p+k) mod2. We want show L_0 = [f=0], L_1 = [f=1].

Consider removal options:
- Even removal (if p<k): leads to (k-1,p,e). Losing iff e = (p+k-1) mod2 = f xor 1 (since k-1 parity opposite k? Actually p+k-1 = f-1 mod2 = f xor1). So even removal is a winning move for current iff e = f xor1.
- Odd removal (if p>0): leads to (k-1,p-1,e xor1). Losing iff e xor1 = (p-1+k-1) mod2 = p+k-2 ≡ f. So odd removal is a winning move iff e xor1 = f, i.e., e = f xor1.
Thus every removal (of either parity, if available) is a winning move exactly when e = f xor1. If e = f, all removals lead to winning states (not losing). If e = f xor1, at least one removal? Need ensure there is at least one removal. If e = f xor1 and no removal? k>=4, p either 0 or k? If p=0 (all even), only even removals exist (p<k true). If p=k (all odd), only odd removals exist (p>0 true). If mixed, both. So at least one removal. Thus:
- If e = f xor1, there is a removal to losing state, so state is winning regardless of pass.
- If e = f, all removals lead to winning. Then outcome depends on pass:
   * If e=0 (f=0): no pass, all moves (removals) lead to winning -> losing.
   * If e=1 (f=1): pass leads to e=0. Need know if e=0 state is losing or winning. But e=0 has f? Since f=1, e=0 = f xor1, and we already determined states with e = f xor1 are winning (by removal). So pass leads to winning. Thus all moves (pass and removals) lead to winning -> losing.
Therefore L_e iff e=f. This is a clean proof! It uses the fact that for e=f xor1, state is winning due to removal, not pass. For e=f, pass leads to e=f xor1 which is winning. No circularity. Great.

This proof actually works for any k where removal losing condition holds for k-1. For k=4, k-1=3 has different L3 table, so base direct. For k>=5, induction. Good.

Let's check the removal losing condition for k-1>=4:
Even removal: resulting p'=p, e'=e. Losing iff e' = (p' + k -1) mod2 = p+k-1 mod2 = f xor1 (since f=p+k, subtract 1 flips). Yes.
Odd removal: p'=p-1, e'=e xor1. Losing iff e xor1 = (p-1 + k -1) = p+k-2 ≡ f. So e = f xor1. Yes.
Thus if e=f xor1, at least one removal of appropriate parity? Suppose e=f xor1. If p<k, even removal exists and leads to losing. If p=k (all odd), p<k false, but p>0 true, odd removal leads to losing? Let's check p=k, f=k+k=0 mod2. e=f xor1=1. Odd removal losing iff e=f xor1=1 yes. So exists. If p=0, even removal exists. Good.
If e=f, all removals lead to winning. Pass if e=1 leads to e=0 which is f xor1 (since f=1), winning. If e=0 no pass. So losing. Perfect.

Thus for k>=4, L iff e=f.

Now, for k=3, we can also solve with k-1=2 table. Let's derive L3 table using same method to ensure our small N code. For k=3, f = p+3 mod2 = p xor1. For k-1=2, L2(p',e') = (e' even) independent of p'. Let's compute removal losing conditions:
- Even removal (if p<3): leads to k=2 p'=p, e'=e. Losing iff e even.
- Odd removal (if p>0): leads to k=2 p'=p-1, e'=e xor1. Losing iff e xor1 even, i.e., e odd.
So if e even, even removal (if exists) is winning move; if e odd, odd removal (if exists) is winning move. Thus:
- If p=0 (all even): even removal exists. For e even, removal to losing -> winning. For e odd, no even? Actually even removal exists but e odd -> leads to winning; odd removal none. Pass? e odd pass to e even. Need determine e even state is winning (just found), so pass leads to winning. Thus e odd all moves to winning -> losing. So L iff e odd. Matches all even.
- If p=3 (all odd): odd removal exists. For e odd, removal to losing -> winning. For e even, odd removal leads to winning; pass? e even no pass? if e=0 no pass, if e=2 pass to e=1 which is winning, so all moves winning -> losing. L iff e even. Matches all odd.
- If p=1 or2 (mixed): both even and odd removals exist. For any e, one of even/odd removal leads to losing? If e even, even removal to losing. If e odd, odd removal to losing. Thus state is always winning. Matches mixed never losing.
Good.

For k=2, using k=1 table (no losing states). Removal always leads to k=1 which is winning for opponent. Thus outcome depends only on pass:
- e=0 no pass, all removals to winning -> losing.
- e=1 pass to e=0 which is losing -> winning.
So L iff e even. Matches.

For k=1, removal to terminal (losing) -> winning always.

Thus complete.

import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    N = data[0]
    A = data[1:]
    even = sum(1 for x in A if x % 2 == 0)
    if N == 1:
        ans = "Fennec"
    elif N == 2:
        ans = "Snuke"
    elif N == 3:
        ans = "Snuke" if even == 3 else "Fennec"
    else:
        ans = "Snuke" if (even % 2) == (N % 2) else "Fennec"
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

```python
import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    N = data[0]
    A = data[1:]
    even = sum(1 for x in A if x % 2 == 0)

    if N == 1:
        ans = "Fennec"
    elif N == 2:
        ans = "Snuke"
    elif N == 3:
        ans = "Snuke" if even == 3 else "Fennec"
    else:
        ans = "Snuke" if (even % 2) == (N % 2) else "Fennec"

    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
